# MAIN clue bank — audit digest

Every category in the shipped bank, with its two titles, the books its clues come from,
and the five clues themselves. English clue text and English answer; the Persian title of
each category is beside it. 731 categories, 3655 clues.

Round rungs: single 200 400 600 800 1000 · double 400 800 1200 1600 2000.

- 1000 — Khomeini's supporters denounced rivals as eclectic and Westoxified, yet coined which Persian neologism by literally translating the English term fundamentalist?  → Bonyadegar

## [single] WHAT'S IN AN IRANIAN NAME?
fa: اسمش را ایران گذاشتند
sources (5): Homa Katouzian, The Persians: Ancient, Mediaeval and Modern  · Eric J. Hooglund, Land and Revolution in Iran, 1960-1980 · Mostafa Vaziri, Iran as Imagined Nation: The Construction of · Mohamad Tavakoli-Targhi, Refashioning Iran: Orientalism, Occidentalis · Abbas Amanat, Iran: A Modern History
- 200 — Completed in 1258 AD, this rhymed prose masterpiece of moral tales and maxims by Sa'di takes its name from the Persian word for 'rose garden'.  → Golestan
- 400 — Hooglund gives this name—literally 'those who sit at ease'—to the roughly 1.4 million landless village families that all three phases of reform left out in the cold. What is the term?  → khwushnishins
- 600 — The author floats this ancient land — the empire of the Arsacids, the Iran that preceded the Sasanians — as a rival candidate for the origin of the name 'Persia'.  → Parthia
- 800 — This Shi'i scholar's 1810 travelogue, Mir'at al-Ahval-i Jahan Nama, devoted a full chapter to 'the classification of the universe according to the school of the philosopher Copernicus.'  → Aqa Ahmad Bihbahani
- 1000 — Amir Naderi's 1973 screen version of this Chubak novel made a folk hero of Behrouz Vossoughi's avenging Shir Mohammad.  → Tangsir

fa: ایران، همین حوالی
sources (5): W. B. Fisher (ed.), The Cambridge History of Iran, Vol. 1: The L · Abbas Amanat, Iran: A Modern History · Homa Katouzian, The Persians: Ancient, Mediaeval and Modern  · Mostafa Vaziri, Iran as Imagined Nation: The Construction of · Afshin Marashi, Nationalizing Iran: Culture, Power, and the 
- 200 — From February to October a steady north-westerly blows off the Tigris-Euphrates valley across the head of the Persian Gulf, at its strongest and steadiest in the hottest summer weeks. Name this wind.  → the shamal
- 400 — Built during the Sasanian era to protect the Caspian frontier from White Hun nomads, this 195-kilometer brick fortification is the Great Wall of this city.  → The Great Wall of Gorgan
- 600 — Woven on traditional wooden looms in the desert city of Yazd, this luxurious hand-woven silk and wool brocade cloth often features the iconic Boteh Jegheh (paisley) motif.  → Termeh
- 800 — During the Sasanian period, in the third century A.D., Islamic geographical sources applied this single term to the whole vast land rather than to one southern province.  → Iranshahr
- 1000 — As the old walls came down and a new perimeter went up, Tehran's population roughly tripled between 1860 and the 1880s, reaching this figure inside the rebuilt walls.  → 150,000

fa: زن‌ها تیتر یک‌اند
sources (5): Asghar Fathi (editor), Women and the Family in Iran · Nazanin Shahrokni, Women in Place: The Politics of Gender Segre · Arang Keshavarzian, Bazaar and State in Iran: The Politics of th · Houchang Chehabi, Government and Opposition · Janet Afary, On the Origins of Feminism in Early 20th-Cen
- 200 — Women's papers multiplied under the young shah: from a mere handful in the 1920s, the count of newspapers and journals by or for women had climbed to this by the early 1950s.  → 23
- 400 — In 2009, President Mahmoud Ahmadinejad broke historical precedent by appointing this gynecologist as the first female cabinet minister in the history of the Islamic Republic, placing her in charge of the Ministry of Health.  → Marzieh Vahid Dastjerdi
- 600 — Azeri speakers would gather at one of the larger houses of worship in the Bazaar, known among traders by this nickname and officially named for Shaykh Abd al-Hosayn.  → the Mosque of the Turks
- 800 — In 1988, reform-oriented clerics broke away from the traditionalist clerical establishment to form this left-leaning association led by Mehdi Karroubi and Mohammad Khatami, creating what Chehabi classifies as an authoritarian 'semi-opposition' favoring economi  → Association of Combatant Clerics
- 1000 — Bibi Khanom's pioneering girls' school Dabestan-e Dushizegan was temporarily forced to close in 1907 after this supreme conservative cleric issued a fatwa declaring girls' education a heresy.  → Shaykh Fazlollah Nuri

## [single] WOMEN OF THE PRESSURE
fa: زن، زندگی، روزنامه
sources (5): Hamideh Sedghi, Women and Politics in Iran: Veiling, Unveili · Firoozeh Kashani-Sabet, British Journal of Middle Eastern Studies · Robin Wright, The Last Great Revolution: Turmoil and Trans · Parvin Paidar, Women and the Political Process in Twentieth · Maziar Behrooz, International Journal of Middle East Studies
- 200 — Sedghi gives this English name to the women's militia, the zanan-e basiji, that scolded, interrogated and intimidated women it judged improperly veiled.  → the gender police
- 400 — In 1922 this periodical reflected on the standing of women in the twentieth century and closed by calling it 'the century of women' (qarn-i zan).  → Bahar
- 600 — Within hours of Parliament approving a sweeping new press law on July 7, 1999, a special court run by the clergy banned this big pro-Khatami daily.  → Salam
- 800 — Soon after founding her paper, Maryam Amid formed this association, the first formal body of its kind in the Qajar period.  → Iranian Women's Society
- 1000 — In the minority on the executive committee, the hard-liners still held this capital-based nerve centre of the party network, run by Amanallah Quraishi.  → the Tehran Provincial Committee

## [single] WOMEN’S WORK IS NEVER GOVERNED
fa: حق با زنان است، حضانت با قانون
sources (4): Shahram Khosravi, Young and Defiant in Tehran · Shirin Saeidi, Women and the Sacred Defense · Valentine M. Moghadam, Third World Quarterly · Shirin Saeidi, Women and the Islamic Republic: How Gendered

- 400 — In the frontline city of Ahvaz, female volunteers performed harrowing labor at this famous facility, washing blood-soaked uniforms and chemical-contaminated blankets sent directly from combat zones.  → Rakhshur-khaneh of Ahvaz
- 600 — UN rapporteur Alena Douhan identified which domestic demographic, increasingly reliant on charities such as Behzisti, as bearing the brunt of food inflation and medical shortages?  → Female-headed households

- 1000 — After the 1988 ceasefire, male veterans received state hiring quotas and pensions, while female wartime volunteers were systematically subjected to this two-part fate.  → Re-domestication and Marginalization

fa: حرفِ حساب، حسابِ حرف
sources (4): Arang Keshavarzian, The Sacred Republic: Power and Institutions  · M. Reza Afshari, The Historians of the Constitutional Movemen · Kamran Talattof, Persian Language, Literature and Culture: Ne · Abbas Amanat, Iran: A Modern History
- 200 — Chanted by students in front of Tehran University during the Dey 2017 protests, this famous rhyming slogan symbolized the total popular rejection of both establishment factions by declaring that 'the game is over'.  → Reformists, principalists, the game is over
- 400 — This leading member of the Secret Society authored *Tarikh-e Bidari-ye Iranian* (History of the Awakening of Iranians), the premier contemporary insider chronicle of the revolution.  → Nazem al Islam Kermani
- 600 — Publishing between 1957 and 1972, which writer became the most significant practitioner of irony in twentieth-century Iranian fiction?  → Bahram Sadeqi
- 800 — Persian scribes crafted luxury carrying cases for their reed pens and inkwells using lacquered papier-mâché painted with nightingales and roses, known by this name.  → Qalamdan (Pen-Box)
- 1000 — This post-revolutionary greeting, literally "may you not be tired", marks a concession the standard language made to the speech of the commoners.  → khasteh nabashid

fa: قلم، راه خودش را می‌رود
sources (4): Kamran Talattof, Persian Language, Literature and Culture: Ne · Homa Katouzian, The Persians: Ancient, Mediaeval and Modern  · Maximilian Terhalle, Security Studies · Abbas Amanat, Iran: A Modern History
- 200 — Jamalzadeh's Once Upon a Time (Yeki bud, yeki nabud), the collection that gave Persian fiction a prose built on everyday speech, appeared in this year.  → 1921
- 400 — An apothecary by trade who perished during the 1221 Mongol sack of Nishapur, this mystic allegorist wrote The Conference of the Birds (Mantiq al-Tayr).  → Attar of Nishapur
- 600 — Which poet and essayist, born in 1894 and killed in 1924, pursued a passionate and self-conscious campaign to modernize Persian poetry?  → 'Eshqi
- 800 — Terhalle evaluates the Islamic Republic's ideological resistance against this constructivist theorist's three degrees of institutional socialization, arguing that revolutionary elites stubbornly refused to internalize international norms.  → Alexander Wendt
- 1000 — Based on a real 1920s incident in Bushehr, this gripping 1963 revenge novel by Sadeq Chubak follows Zar Mohammad as he methodically murders the four corrupt notables who defrauded him.  → Tangsir
